This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   This session handled five Director instructions for the `recursive_gen_depth_est` EMNLP 2026 paper:
   
   **Task A (Dense Figure 1)**: Run PaperBanana with high-information-density intent for Figure 1. Three panels: (a) Recursive Generation Chain with LLM θ badges, subtitles, gradient bar; (b) Depth Estimation Pipeline with 19 features in 2×2 grid, Random Forest dark box, bar chart, inset tail decay chart; (c) Task Comparison with binary vs ordinal + 78.4% acc stat. 5 rounds of external iteration completed. Dense v5 confirmed by user/Director.
   
   **Task B (Related Work Trim Round 1)**: Paper exceeded 8-page limit by ~10 lines. Cut Related Work: removed mitigation/convergence survey sentence (14 citations), SeqXGPT + shared tasks, compressed Membership paragraph. Result: 28→27 pages.
   
   **Task C (Related Work Trim Round 2)**: Still 4 lines over. Further trimmed: AI Text Detection citation list (-3 refs), Ordinal Classification merged to 1 sentence, Decoding Effects merged to 1 sentence. Also compressed Bertrand sentence and merged AI Text Detection conclusion sentences. Result: 27→26 pages (main text + Limitations fully on page 8).
   
   **Task D (Expand Main Text)**: Over-trimmed, page 8 had whitespace. Expanded Method §3.4 (feature design rationale connecting to tail erosion theory) and Discussion §6 (Practical Implications paragraph + expanded Open Questions).      The design targets the signal predicted by model collapse theory: recursive finetuning progressively erodes high-perplexity tokens \cite{shumailov2024_nature}, so upper quantiles (p75, p90) and kurtosis track tail erosion monotonically across depths (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
     Surprisal variance captures a complementary effect, dominating at shallow depths where tail erosion is still subtle (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}); lexical and repetition features improve cross-strategy robustness despite limited individual discriminative power (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_ablation}).

     The lightweight feature extraction (19 statistics from a single reference-model forward pass) makes inline deployment practical for provenance auditing.

        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}\nRecursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, an effect formalized as exponential tail decay via scaling laws \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails,seddik2024synthetic} and extended to autophagous loops in the MAD framework \\cite{alemohammad2024_mad} and Markovian generation chains \\cite{geng2026_markovian}.\n\\citet{bertrand2024_stability} identify convergence to a fixed point under mixed real-synthetic retraining (grounding our EDD ceiling, \\S\\ref{sec:method:theory}); refinements address generalization-to-memorization transitions \\cite{shi2025_closer_look} and broader model classes \\cite{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse}.\n\\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple phenomena; we respond by operationalizing tail-erosion into a deployable ordinal classifier that reveals strategy-dependent trajectories the theory does not predict.\nAll assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}\nThe detection literature \\cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt,fast_detectgpt2024,binoculars2024,gehrmann2019_gltr,verma2024_ghostbuster}, geometric \\cite{tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} methods, with known limits \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase,pudasaini2026_detection_fails}.\n\\citet{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms} distinguish attribution from verification; all remain binary, whereas our approach outputs ordinal depth labels along a third axis, recursive iteration.\n\n\\paragraph{Membership Inference and Data Provenance.}\nTraining data extraction \\cite{carlini2021_extracting} motivates provenance pipelines; Min-K\\% Prob \\cite{shi2024_minkprob} and Datamodels \\cite{ilyas2022datamodels} address membership inference but remain binary; watermarks \\cite{christ2024_undetectable} require generator cooperation; TROVE \\cite{zhu2025_trove} traces sentence-level provenance but targets source attribution.\nDepth estimation occupies a distinct niche: ordinal, retroactive, and cooperation-free.\n\n\\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent; \\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \\emph{which} model generated text; \\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} characterize \\emph{how} text degrades under iterative generation; none address ordinal depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}\nCORAL \\cite{coral2020} and CLOC \\cite{cloc2025} enforce ordinal rank consistency via ordered binary tasks and contrastive losses, respectively; we adopt both as baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}\nDecoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}; our atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) confirms they induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories.",

        "content": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nFour importance measures converge on the same ranking (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$); Binoculars cross-perplexity features independently reach 78.1\\% (merged: 82.6\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation_bino}); four classifiers converge within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); CORAL adds $+$0.3\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}).\nThe accuracy ceiling thus resides in the feature space, not the classifier.\nEnd-to-end classifiers lag by 14--33\\,pp (d2 F1\\,${\\leq}$\\,0.26; Appendix~\\ref{app:e2e}, \\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), likely because depth signal resides in passage-level distributional tail statistics rather than local coherence patterns.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeds the model effect (7.2\\,pp), so deployment requires conditioning on decoding configuration (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}); the fixed reference model degrades by 4--11\\,pp under cross-architecture alternatives (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), and a 7B self-reference probe reveals a scale-dependent ceiling ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:qwen7b_selfref}).\nAccumulation experiments and cross-boundary chains (84.8\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:accumulation_auc},~\\ref{app:cross_boundary}) indicate that depth fingerprints are process-level rather than model-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nOrdinal depth labels enable tiered data curation that binary detection cannot support: a pipeline can retain first-generation text ($d{=}1$), which may still carry training value, while filtering $d{\\geq}2$ content where collapse compounds.\nSince binary detectors collapse all $d{\\geq}1$ with chance-level pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), they cannot implement such depth-aware policies.\nThe lightweight feature extraction (19 statistics from a single reference-model forward pass) makes inline deployment practical for provenance auditing.\n\nIn summary, recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints that a lightweight pipeline resolves into ordinal depth classes ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells), enabling more targeted data filtering than binary detection for provenance auditing and collapse monitoring.\nOpen questions include reference-model alignment beyond 1.5B (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), paraphrase robustness, finer-grained mixing (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:accumulation}), and extension to instruction-tuned models.\n",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nWe use \\emph{depth estimation} as shorthand throughout; the underlying task is ordinal classification.\nDepths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K cell-instances; 140K unique passages, as d0 samples are drawn from a shared Wikipedia seed corpus across all cells).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend all three models under nucleus sampling to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}; cross-seed replication in Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBOS seeding may amplify depth signal; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and Variance Inflation Factor (VIF) analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llr}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio (TTR), hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\nThe design targets the signal predicted by model collapse theory: recursive finetuning progressively erodes high-perplexity tokens \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, so upper quantiles (p75, p90) and kurtosis track tail erosion monotonically across depths (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\nSurprisal variance captures a complementary effect, dominating at shallow depths where tail erosion is still subtle (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}); lexical and repetition features improve cross-strategy robustness despite limited individual discriminative power (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation}).\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation (CV).\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in the primary benchmark decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles occur only in cross-scale extensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows $\\mathrm{EDD}$ shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees; seed robustness in Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_seed}).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89.\nComparisons against other classifiers and detection baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",
        "numLines": 110,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 110
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
  },

        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur benchmark uses full-replacement chains at 1--1.5B parameters; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios (per-passage robustness in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_depth}), and depth signal attenuates at larger scales (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), with reference-model mismatch contributing to the observed degradation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nCross-strategy transfer can invert adjacent-depth predictions, requiring strategy-aware deployment (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nOn held-out web-crawl corpora, pooled-classifier raw false-positive rates range from 15\\% to 34\\%, reducible to ${\\leq}$7\\% via posterior-threshold calibration (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}); robustness to post-hoc paraphrasing remains untested.\n",
        "numLines": 7,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 7

Prior z-score recovery (0.13→0.68) was with LR, not RF.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"notes\": \"Z-score standardization has negligible effect on RF cross-strategy LOO. temp09 d1v2 AUC remains inverted: GPT-2 XL 0.1288→0.1348, OLMo 0.2529→0.2497, Pythia 0.576→0.594. Root cause: RF is a tree-based model invariant to monotonic feature transforms — z-score shifts/scales features but preserves relative ordering, so split thresholds adapt automatically. Prior independent verification (GPT-2 XL 0.13→0.68, OLMo 0.25→0.83) was with a scale-sensitive classifier (LR), not RF. Raw results match cross_strategy_loo_001 exactly (mean acc=0.5908). Non-temp09 impact negligible (mean Δd1v2=+0.006). [Director 指令] 用户要求做匿名仓库重整 + 论文链接位置规范化。两部分任务。

## Part 1 — 匿名仓库重整

仓库地址：https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/recursive_gen_depth_est
Push URL 和 token 在 `_anonymous_repo.json` 中。

### 1.1 验证密钥
用 `_anonymous_repo.json` 里的 push_url 做 `git push --dry-run` 确认 token 有效。

### 1.2 仓库内容自检
- **匿名性**：commit author/committer 必须是 anonymous（不能有真实姓名/邮箱/机构名）。README / LICENSE / 代码注释 / requirements.txt 里不能有真名、机构名、师生关系暗示
- **完整性**：README.md 写清楚怎么跑（环境、依赖、入口脚本、数据获取）；核心代码、训练/评测脚本、关键 config 都在；requirements.txt 能 reproduce
- **干净度**：删除 .DS_Store、__pycache__、*.pyc、.env、wandb/、临时实验日志、未引用的死代码
- **数据可获取**：自定义数据集在 README 写明获取方式

### 1.3 重新推送
自检修复后 force-push 到匿名仓库。Author 设为 Anonymous。

## Part 2 — 论文链接位置规范化

### 规则
- **位置**：第一页脚注（footnote），挂在 Abstract 末尾
- **禁止**：放在 Appendix、References、论文末尾
- **只放一处**，不要 abstract 一遍 intro 又一遍 appendix 再一遍

### 标准 LaTeX 格式
在 Abstract 最后一句末尾加：
```latex
We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/recursive_gen_depth_est}}.
```

### 检查现有链接
搜索论文所有 .tex 文件中已有的仓库链接引用，如果有重复出现在其他位置（intro、appendix 等），删除多余的，只保留 Abstract 脚注这一处。

## 输出要求
编译确认无错误，发 PDF 到群里 + 同步匿名仓库。

汇报：
(a) 密钥是否有效
(b) 仓库重新推送完成时间戳
(c) 论文链接放在第几页/哪个章节/用了哪个模板
(d) 匿名性确认（commit author / README 等）

The abstract.tex file contains (lines 1-9):

```
\begin{abstract}
As language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K cell-instances (140K unique passages) spanning three model families, three decoding strategies, and four depths under full-replacement chains at 1--1.5B scale.
A lightweight pipeline (19 statistical features classified by Random Forest) achieves d1--d3 balanced accuracy of 59--82\% across nine model--strategy cells, with Effective Discriminable Depth $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} = 3$ in 8/9 cells.
Three findings emerge: (1)~depth signal concentrates in perplexity distribution tails, corroborated by classifier convergence and independent cross-perplexity features; (2)~decoding strategy affects cross-condition transfer more than model architecture, consistent with structurally distinct degradation trajectories; (3)~signal attenuates with scale (14B: $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$ under nucleus and temperature, 0 under top-$k$), with reference-model choice as a major confound.
Our claims hold within-strategy or when decoding configuration is known; a two-stage routing pipeline recovers $+$11.5\,pp under cross-strategy transfer.
Perturbation-based and membership-inference baselines lack ordinal resolution; depth estimation requires distributional statistics beyond binary detection scores.
\end{abstract}
```

**Key Finding**: The abstract currently contains NO footnote with a repository link. The paper is in anonymous submission form. You have full flexibility to add a footnote with the anonymous repository URL.

1	\begin{abstract}
2	As language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K cell-instances (140K unique passages) spanning three model families, three decoding strategies, and four depths under full-replacement chains at 1--1.5B scale.
4	A lightweight pipeline (19 statistical features classified by Random Forest) achieves d1--d3 balanced accuracy of 59--82\% across nine model--strategy cells, with Effective Discriminable Depth $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} = 3$ in 8/9 cells.
5	Three findings emerge: (1)~depth signal concentrates in perplexity distribution tails, corroborated by classifier convergence and independent cross-perplexity features; (2)~decoding strategy affects cross-condition transfer more than model architecture, consistent with structurally distinct degradation trajectories; (3)~signal attenuates with scale (14B: $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$ under nucleus and temperature, 0 under top-$k$), with reference-model choice as a major confound.
6	Our claims hold within-strategy or when decoding configuration is known; a two-stage routing pipeline recovers $+$11.5\,pp under cross-strategy transfer.
7	Perturbation-based and membership-inference baselines lack ordinal resolution; depth estimation requires distributional statistics beyond binary detection scores.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

_source line 5451 · 2026-05-25T08:46:38.749Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01225tyuKYSwxx8zRs6QyVXX`</summary>

````text
1	# Recursive Generation Depth Estimation
2	
3	Code for "Recursive Generation Depth Estimation: How Many Times Has This Text Been Through the Loop?"
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	This repository contains code for estimating the ordinal recursive generation depth (d0-d3) of text passages using statistical features and Random Forest classification.
8	
9	## Structure
10	
11	- `src/` — Core pipeline: feature extraction, atlas construction, evaluation
12	- `scripts/` — Training, generation, baseline evaluation scripts  
13	- `artifacts/` — Experiment-specific scripts (baselines, ablations, analysis)
14	- `figures/` — Paper figure generation scripts
15	
16	## Requirements
17	
18	- Python 3.10+
19	- PyTorch 2.0+
20	- transformers, datasets, scikit-learn, scipy, numpy, pandas
21	
22	Install dependencies:
23	```bash
24	pip install torch transformers datasets scikit-learn scipy numpy pandas tqdm wandb peft
25	```
26	
27	## Pipeline
28	
29	1. **Data generation**: `scripts/lora_finetune.py` → `scripts/generate_next_depth.py` (repeat for each depth)
30	2. **Feature extraction & classification**: `src/atlas_v2.py`
31	3. **Evaluation**: `src/binary_pairwise_eval.py` (pairwise AUC / EDD computation)
32	
33	## Baselines
34	
35	- DeBERTa end-to-end: `src/n2_deberta_finetune.py`
36	- CORAL ordinal regression: `scripts/baseline_coral.py`
37	- DetectGPT / Binoculars / Min-K%: `scripts/detection_baselines_ordinal.py`
38	- Zero-shot LLM: `scripts/baseline_zeroshot_llm.py`
39	- Perplexity regression: `scripts/baseline_ppl_regression.py`
40	
41	## License
42	
43	MIT
44	
````

</details>

_source line 5509 · 2026-05-25T09:06:09.145Z_

[Director 指令] 用户要求做附录内容引用完整性检查。

## 任务

### 1. 逐项盘点附录
读 `docs/paper/section_appendix.tex`，列出每一个 section / subsection / table / figure / algorithm。

### 2. 逐项验证引用
对每一项，在正文所有 .tex 文件中搜索是否有指向它的引用（如 "see Appendix A"、"Table X in Appendix"、\ref{} 等）。

不合格的引用：
- 只在末尾甩一句 "See appendix for more details"（没指向具体 section）
- 只在 Limitations/Conclusion 才提
- 引用了但没说明为什么读者要去看

### 3. 修复
- **优选**：在最相关的正文章节加一句自然的引用，说明读者去看什么、解决什么疑问
- **次选**：附录内容对核心 claim 不必要 → 删除
- **不要**：硬塞一行 "See Appendix X." 当补丁

### 4. 引用质量
每条引用要让 reviewer 明白：为什么要去翻、能找到什么（表/图/proof/例子）

### 5. 约束
- 禁止 em dash
- 修改后编译确认无错误
- 主文仍然 ≤ 8 页（加引用句不能撑爆页面）
- 发 PDF 到群里 + 同步匿名仓库

### 汇报
(a) 附录有多少个 section/subsection？分别是什么内容？
(b) 哪些原本没有正文引用？现在加在了哪个章节？
(c) 是否有附录内容被删除？

```text
1	\section{LoRA Fine-Tuning Protocol}
2	\label{app:lora_protocol}
3	
4	Table~\ref{tab:lora_protocol} lists the full LoRA fine-tuning configuration used for recursive chain construction (\S\ref{sec:method:benchmark}).
5	At each depth $k$, a fresh LoRA adapter is initialized on the base model and trained on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$; adapters are not inherited across depths.
6	The HuggingFace Trainer default linear decay schedule is used with no warmup.
7	Target modules are model-specific: \texttt{query\_key\_value} (Pythia), \texttt{att\_proj} (OLMo), and \texttt{c\_attn} (GPT-2~XL).
8	Cross-scale 7--8B experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) use Quantized LoRA (QLoRA; 4-bit NF4) with $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$; the 14B model uses FP16 LoRA with the same rank and scaling.
9	
10	\begin{table}[h]
11	\centering
12	\small
13	\caption{LoRA fine-tuning hyperparameters for the primary 1--1.5B benchmark. Identical across all three model families and all depth levels.}
14	\label{tab:lora_protocol}
15	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
16	\toprule
17	Parameter & Value \\
18	\midrule
19	LoRA rank $r$ & 16 \\
20	LoRA alpha $\alpha$ & 32 \\
21	LoRA dropout & 0.05 \\
22	Bias & none \\
23	Epochs per depth & 3 \\
24	Learning rate & $2 \times 10^{-4}$ \\
25	LR schedule & linear decay (no warmup) \\
26	Batch size (effective) & 16 ($8 \times 2$ grad.\ accum.) \\
27	Max sequence length & 512 tokens \\
28	Precision & bfloat16 \\
29	Adapter init & from scratch (base model) \\
30	Seed & 42 (primary), 43 (replication) \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	\section{Additional Results}
36	\label{app:additional}
37	
38	\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}
39	\label{app:topk_trajectories}
40	
41	Feature trajectories for nucleus and temperature sampling appear in Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
42	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
43	Concretely, the d0$\to$d1 drop in \texttt{p90\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\% (vs.\ 87\% for nucleus), and the d2$\to$d3 reduction is 31\% (vs.\ 39\% for nucleus).
44	Despite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.
45	
46	\subsection{Permutation Importance}
47	\label{app:permutation}
48	
49	To verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.
50	The top-2 features are stable across measures: \texttt{p90\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both Gini and permutation importance, and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$; the single SHAP exception is GPT-2~XL top-$k$, where \texttt{p75\_ppl} displaces \texttt{var\_surprisal} to rank 3).
51	Kendall's $W{=}0.90$ across all three methods (Gini, permutation, SHAP) indicates strong concordance ($p{<}0.005$).
52	Pairwise Spearman correlations are: SHAP--Gini $\rho{=}0.93$, SHAP--permutation $\rho{=}0.84$, Gini--permutation $\rho{=}0.76$.
53	Per-cell top-5 overlap between SHAP and permutation importance is 3--4 out of 5 features; the aggregated (mean-across-cells) top-5 sets are identical; the top features are therefore robust to the choice of importance measure at the aggregate level, though per-cell rankings exhibit model-dependent variation.
54	
55	\subsection{Conditional Permutation Importance under Multicollinearity}
56	\label{app:conditional_pi}
57	
58	With 14--16 of 19 features exhibiting VIF${>}$10 (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}), standard permutation importance may inflate contributions of correlated features: permuting one member of a collinear set allows others to compensate, masking true individual effects.
59	We compute conditional permutation importance (CPI) \citep{strobl2008_conditional}, which permutes each feature \emph{within strata defined by its correlated partners}, isolating the unique contribution after controlling for multicollinearity.
60	
61	\paragraph{Setup.}
62	CPI is computed on all 9 model$\times$strategy cells.
63	For each cell, 1{,}000 bootstrap resamples produce a rank distribution per feature; the 95\% bootstrap CI width quantifies rank stability (width $\leq 1.0$ indicates near-invariant rankings across resamples).
64	
65	\paragraph{Results.}
66	Table~\ref{tab:conditional_pi} summarizes the top-5 features by mean CPI across cells.
67	\texttt{var\_surprisal} (avg rank 1.4, CPI\,=\,0.260) and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (avg rank 1.7, CPI\,=\,0.238) are the top-2 in 8/9 cells, with bootstrap rank CI widths of 0.44 and 0.22 (both $\leq 0.5$), indicating near-invariant rankings across resamples.
68	Ranks 3--5 are \texttt{p75\_ppl} (CPI\,=\,0.063, CI width\,=\,0.78), \texttt{p50\_ppl} (0.016, width\,=\,2.67), and a cell-dependent set (\texttt{p25\_ppl} or \texttt{mean\_ppl}); rank stability decreases beyond the top-2, with CI widths of 2.7--3.7 for ranks 4--5.
69	
70	\begin{table}[h]
71	\centering
72	\small
73	\caption{Conditional permutation importance aggregated across 9 model$\times$strategy cells (1{,}000 bootstrap resamples). Avg Rank and Avg CPI: means over cells. Rank CI Width: mean 95\% bootstrap CI width for the rank. Bottom rows: feature-family averages.}
74	\label{tab:conditional_pi}
75	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
76	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
77	\toprule
78	Feature / Family & Avg Rank & Avg CPI & Rank CI Width \\
79	\midrule
80	\texttt{var\_surprisal} & 1.4 & 0.260 & 0.44 \\
81	\texttt{p90\_ppl} & 1.7 & 0.238 & 0.22 \\
82	\texttt{p75\_ppl} & 3.1 & 0.063 & 0.78 \\
83	\texttt{p50\_ppl} & 5.0 & 0.016 & 2.67 \\
84	\texttt{p25\_ppl}$^\dagger$ & 5.8 & 0.012 & 3.67 \\
85	\texttt{mean\_ppl}$^\dagger$ & 6.3 & 0.008 & 3.44 \\
86	\midrule
87	Surprisal family & 7.3 & 0.089 & --- \\
88	Perplexity family & 7.5 & 0.039 & --- \\
89	Lexical family & 9.7 & 0.004 & --- \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$Rank 5--6 varies by cell; \texttt{trigram\_entropy} appears in 1 cell.}
92	\end{tabular}}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	Two exceptions exist among GPT-2~XL cells: in temperature sampling, \texttt{p90\_ppl} (CPI\,=\,0.419) exceeds \texttt{var\_surprisal} (0.093) while both remain in the top-2; in top-$k$ sampling, \texttt{p90\_ppl} (0.475) and \texttt{p75\_ppl} (0.127) push \texttt{var\_surprisal} to rank~3 (0.037), the only cell where it exits the top-2.
96	Both reversals are consistent with the Gini rankings for these cells (Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance}).
97	
98	At the family level, surprisal (avg CPI\,=\,0.089, rank\,=\,7.3) and perplexity (0.039, rank\,=\,7.5) are closely matched, while lexical features (0.004, rank\,=\,9.7) contribute minimally, consistent with the group ablation in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}.
99	
100	\paragraph{Consistency with Gini rankings.}
101	CPI and Gini rankings agree on the dominant feature (\texttt{var\_surprisal} or \texttt{p90\_ppl}) in 8/9 cells; the sole disagreement (GPT-2~XL top-$k$) involves a swap between rank-2 and rank-3 features.
102	Bootstrap rank CI widths $\leq 0.5$ for the top-2 features confirm that these rankings are not artifacts of collinearity or resampling variability.
103	Controlling for multicollinearity via conditional permutation corroborates the Gini- and SHAP-based importance hierarchy reported in the main text.
104	
105	\subsection{Per-Cell Gini Feature Rankings}
106	\label{app:gini_per_cell}
107

132	\label{app:per_class}
133

249	\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}
250	\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}
251	
252	Both DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.
253	The system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).
254	The user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.
255	No in-context examples are provided.
256	
257	\begin{quote}
258	\small
259	\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\ldots]''
260	
261	\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \{text\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \{\texttt{"depth"}: $\langle$int 0--3$\rangle$, \texttt{"confidence"}: $\langle$float 0--1$\rangle$, \texttt{"reasoning"}: $\langle$str$\rangle$\}''
262	\end{quote}
263	
264	\subsection{Temperature Robustness}
265	\label{app:multi_tau}
266	
267	The cross-strategy results in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$).
268	To test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\tau \in \{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\}$ on Pythia-1.4B.
269	
270	\paragraph{Within-$\tau$ robustness.}
271	For $\tau \in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\% to 77.3\% (Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of a single temperature.
272	The same top-5 features (\texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}) dominate across all $\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.
273	
274	\paragraph{Collapse at $\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}
275	At $\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.
276	Training loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \to 0.42 \to 0.16$), a sign of rapid memorization of the model's own output.
277	At $\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \to 1.91 \to 1.85$); high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.
278	This establishes \emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.
279	
280	\paragraph{Cross-$\tau$ non-transferability.}
281	Leave-one-$\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross}), worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\%; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
282	Holdout $\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176); different temperatures therefore produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.
283	This extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific; strategy-conditioned classifiers are therefore necessary.
284	
285	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
286	On WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\% domain shift in mean perplexity, partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.
287	On noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.
288	
289	\begin{table}[h]
290	\centering
291	\small
292	\caption{Within-$\tau$ 4-class classification (Pythia-1.4B, 5-fold CV, independent pipeline run). d$i$v$j$: pairwise AUC for depth $i$ vs.\ depth $j$. The $\tau{=}0.9$ accuracy (75.9\%) differs from the main atlas (76.8\%, Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix}) due to separate generation and LoRA training runs.}
293	\label{tab:multi_tau_within}
294	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
295	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
296	\toprule
297	$\tau$ & 4-class acc (\%) & d1v2 AUC & d0v1 AUC & d2v3 AUC \\
298	\midrule
299	0.5 & 77.3 & 0.842 & 0.996 & 0.764 \\
300	0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\

325	
326	\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}
327	\label{app:feature_overlap}
328	
329	We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.
330	PLM representations excel at surface lexical features (type--token ratio (TTR): $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics; \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing; these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.
331	This confirms that the 20pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary, not redundant, representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
332	
333	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
334	\label{app:e2e}
335	
336	DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\% ($\pm$4.7\%) and 59.5\% ($\pm$3.2\%) respectively, lagging the RF baseline (78.7\%) by 20.4pp and 19.2pp.
337	The 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode (high d0 F1, $>$0.94, but collapsed d1/d2 performance, F1 $\leq$ 0.42), while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.
338	
339	\begin{table}[h]
340	\centering
341	\small
342	\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}
343	\label{tab:e2e_detail}
344	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
345	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
346	\toprule
347	Method & Acc (\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\
348	\midrule
349	DeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\,$\pm$\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\
350	RoBERTa-base & 59.5\,$\pm$\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\
351	\midrule
352	RF (19 feat.) & 78.7 & --- & --- & .596$^\dagger$ & --- \\
353	\bottomrule
354	\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}
355	\end{tabular}}
356	\end{table}
357	
358	\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}
359	SHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\% of RF's total contribution.
360	Three independent importance measures (SHAP, Gini, and permutation) show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \S\ref{app:permutation}), with \texttt{p90\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9.
361	Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (\S\ref{app:feature_overlap}); pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics, a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.
362	
363	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (\S\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
364	This comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.
365	
366	\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}
367	\label{app:domain_bias}
368	
369	To assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.
370	
371	\paragraph{Methodological note.}
372	An initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\%).
373	We discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.
374	The results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.
375	
376	\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}
377	Five-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\% to 82\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\times$ above the 33.3\% chance level); depth signal is therefore the dominant classification factor.
378	
379	\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}
380	We assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.
381	LOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\% (range 59.5--81.6\%), matching standard CV (72.0\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).
382	Per-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp; cross-group generalization is therefore as reliable as within-group classification.
383	
384	\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}
385	We train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\%).
386	Depth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\%, indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).
387	The depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\times$; the feature representation therefore encodes depth but not seed-article identity.
388	
389	Taken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.
390

cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. Boldface marks the highest d2 recall.}\n\\label{tab:per_class_recall}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc.       & 99.3 & 81.9 & 59.2 & 74.4 \\\\\nPythia, $\\tau$     & 99.2 & 79.9 & 56.0 & 72.1 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$    & 98.7 & 71.6 & 44.2 & 60.9 \\\\\nOLMo, nuc.         & 97.8 & 82.1 & 60.6 & 75.3 \\\\\nOLMo, $\\tau$       & 99.3 & 87.1 & 65.6 & 77.1 \\\\\nOLMo, top-$k$      & 99.2 & 80.9 & 50.4 & 67.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, nuc.     & 98.5 & 87.4 & 72.8 & 78.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$   & 99.0 & 91.1 & \\textbf{73.0} & 80.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, top-$k$  & 98.7 & 81.3 & 54.8 & 68.4 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Confusion Matrices}\n\\label{app:confusion_matrices}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices: best in-distribution (GPT-2 XL temp, 85.9\\%) and best leave-one-out (LOO) transfer (OLMo, 69.2\\%). d0 recall ${>}$99\\%; errors spread to d1--d3 under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer Details}\n\\label{app:cross_strategy}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_transfer_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model vs.\\ cross-strategy transfer accuracy (leave-one-out). Strategy holdout range ($\\Delta{=}11.6$\\,pp) exceeds model holdout range ($\\Delta{=}6.8$\\,pp); decoding configuration is the primary transfer barrier.}\n\\label{fig:transfer_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Accumulation Pairwise AUC}\n\\label{app:accumulation_auc}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:accum_auc} reports pairwise AUC for the six accumulation cells (3 models $\\times$ 2 mixing ratios; GPT-2~XL and Pythia use nucleus, OLMo uses temperature 0.9).\nAt 25\\% mixing, all three cells preserve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$.\nAt 50\\% mixing, GPT-2~XL nucleus is the sole failure: d2v3 AUC\\,=\\,0.695, marginally below the 0.70 threshold (0.55\\,pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise AUC under accumulation (GPT-2~XL/Pythia: nucleus; OLMo: temp 0.9; binary RF, 5-fold CV). Bold: $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ boundary failure.}\n\\label{tab:accum_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Mix & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL & 25\\% & 1.000 & 0.817 & 0.726 \\\\\n & 50\\% & 1.000 & 0.831 & \\textbf{0.695} \\\\\nPythia & 25\\% & 1.000 & 0.809 & 0.749 \\\\\n & 50\\% & 1.000 & 0.793 & 0.716 \\\\\nOLMo & 25\\% & 1.000 & 0.848 & 0.759 \\\\\n & 50\\% & 1.000 & 0.817 & 0.726 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}\n\\label{app:dcol_iterations}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.\nDeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.7\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}\n\\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}\n\nBoth DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.\nThe system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).\nThe user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.\nNo in-context examples are provided.\n\n\\begin{quote}\n\\small\n\\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\\ldots]''\n\n\\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \\{text\\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \\{\\texttt{\"depth\"}: $\\langle$int 0--3$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"confidence\"}: $\\langle$float 0--1$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"reasoning\"}: $\\langle$str$\\rangle$\\}''\n\\end{quote}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Robustness}\n\\label{app:multi_tau}\n\nThe cross-strategy results in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$).\nTo test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\\tau \\in \\{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\\}$ on Pythia-1.4B.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-$\\tau$ robustness.}\nFor $\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\\% to 77.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of a single temperature.\nThe same top-5 features (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}) dominate across all $\\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.\nTraining loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \\to 0.42 \\to 0.16$), a sign of rapid memorization of the model's own output.\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \\to 1.91 \\to 1.85$); high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes \\emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$\\tau$ non-transferability.}\nLeave-one-$\\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross}), worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nHoldout $\\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176); different temperatures therefore produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.\nThis extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific; strategy-conditioned classifiers are therefore necessary.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity, partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-$\\tau$ 4-class classification (Pythia-1.4B, 5-fold CV, independent pipeline run). d$i$v$j$: pairwise AUC for depth $i$ vs.\\ depth $j$. The $\\tau{=}0.9$ accuracy (75.9\\%) differs from the main atlas (76.8\\%, Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix}) due to separate generation and LoRA training runs.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_within}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ & 4-class acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC & d0v1 AUC & d2v3 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 77.3 & 0.842 & 0.996 & 0.764 \\\\\n0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\\\\n0.9 & 75.9 & 0.808 & 0.991 & 0.781 \\\\\n1.1 & 57.2 & 0.528 & 0.988 & 0.599 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-$\\tau$ leave-one-out transfer (Pythia-1.4B). Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (type--token ratio (TTR): $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing; these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 20pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary, not redundant, representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively, lagging the RF baseline (78.7\\%) by 20.4pp and 19.2pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode (high d0 F1, $>$0.94, but collapsed d1/d2 performance, F1 $\\leq$ 0.42), while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.7 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures (SHAP, Gini, and permutation) show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}); pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics, a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level); depth signal is therefore the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp; cross-group generalization is therefore as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%, indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$; the feature representation therefore encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:calibration}",

413	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\
414	\bottomrule
415	\end{tabular}}
416	\end{table}
417	
418	On WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy; editorial text is therefore reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.
419	On C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\%) vs.\ Pythia (20.2\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\%).
420	C4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.
421	The model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.
422	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%). The non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
423	
424	\paragraph{Threshold calibration.}
425	A pooled RF classifier (trained on all three families' nucleus data) achieves Expected Calibration Error (ECE)\,=\,0.052 on in-distribution data; Platt scaling and isotonic regression yield no improvement; probability estimates are already well calibrated.
426	The high FPR on web-crawl data is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a probability calibration defect.
427	Applying a confidence threshold $P(\mathrm{d0}) \geq 0.15$ to the pooled classifier reduces its C4 FPR from 33.4\% (not shown in Table~\ref{tab:calibration}) to 2.8\%, at a cost of only $-$0.3\,pp balanced accuracy (78.0\%$\to$77.7\%).
428	The $P(\mathrm{d0})$ distribution is bimodal: 67\% of samples exceed 0.95 (high-confidence d0), while 33\% fall in the 0.15--0.30 uncertainty zone; the threshold separates these modes cleanly.
429	Wild-data FPR is thus an operational threshold choice rather than a fundamental limitation, though the optimal threshold is domain-dependent.
430	
431	\paragraph{Additional web corpora.}
432	Evaluating the pooled classifier on FineWeb (10{,}000 passages) and RedPajama (10{,}000 passages) yields raw FPR of 17.2\% and 15.5\%, respectively, both below C4 (34.0\%), likely reflecting less template-heavy content in these corpora.
433	Posterior-threshold calibration ($P(\mathrm{d0}) \geq 0.10$) reduces FPR to 0.1\% (FineWeb) and 7.0\% (RedPajama); across all three web-crawl corpora, calibrated FPR remains ${\leq}$7\%.
434

509	\subsection{Cross-Scale Full Results}
510	\label{app:cross_scale_full}
511	
512	This section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.
513	We extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct \cite{qwen2.5}, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3}) and one 14B model (Qwen2.5-14B).
514	The 7--8B models use 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, depths $d \in \{0, 1, \ldots, 5\}$).
515	The 14B model uses clean FP16 LoRA fine-tuning across all three decoding strategies (nucleus, temperature $\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$; 5{,}000 samples per depth, depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$).
516	Feature extraction for 1--8B models uses Pythia-1.4B as reference; Qwen2.5-14B is evaluated under both self-reference and cross-reference (Pythia-1.4B) to disentangle reference-model artifacts from scale effects (see paragraph~5 below).
517	
518	\paragraph{Scale comparison.}
519	Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the 7--14B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (depths 0--3).
520	The 14B model is tested across all three decoding strategies; the 7--8B models use nucleus sampling only.

548	\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}
549	Both 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\% (95\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$); recursive depth therefore remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\times$ larger than the reference model.
550	Depth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).
551	
552	\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}
553	At threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} = 5$ (the maximum observable from the tested depth range), exceeding the $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.
554	Llama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.
555	Both larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).
556
