576	
577	\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories and instruction-tuning effect.}
578	Among instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).
579	A base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\% four-class accuracy (95\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\% with strictly monotonic decay.
580	The base-model control experiment suggests instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds the depth signal.
581	Separately, the FP16 self-reference experiment at 7B (Appendix~\ref{app:qwen7b_selfref}) produces strictly monotonic AUC decay (0.697, 0.673, 0.659), indicating that the U-shaped pattern in QLoRA Qwen2.5-7B likely originates from the cross-reference setup (Pythia-1.4B as observer) rather than from an intrinsic property of the depth signal.
582	
583	\begin{table}[h]
584	\centering
585	\small
586	\caption{Pairwise AUC: Llama-3.1-8B Base vs.\ Instruct.}
587	\label{tab:llama3_base_auc}
588	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
589	\toprule
590	Variant & $d_{0\text{v}1}$ & $d_{1\text{v}2}$ & $d_{2\text{v}3}$ & $d_{3\text{v}4}$ & $d_{4\text{v}5}$ \\
591	\midrule
592	Base & 0.918 & 0.794 & 0.682 & 0.730 & 0.595 \\
593	Instruct & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\
594	\bottomrule
595	\end{tabular}
596	\end{table}
597	
598	The base model exhibits non-monotonic decay with a U-shaped recovery at $d_{3\text{v}4}$, while the instruction-tuned variant shows strictly monotonic decay. Both variants exceed chance (AUC $> 0.5$) at all boundaries; depth signal exists regardless of instruction-tuning, though instruction-tuning strengthens and regularizes it.
599	
600	\paragraph{(4) Signal attenuation at 14B.}
601	Qwen2.5-14B (clean FP16, self-reference) is tested across all three decoding strategies (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale}).
602	Mean four-class accuracy is 50.8\% ($2.0{\times}$ chance), with nucleus 54.1\%, temperature 50.5\%, and top-$k$ 47.9\%.
603	$\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$ across all three strategies because the d0/d1 boundary falls below the 0.70 AUC threshold in every case (0.56--0.64), breaking the contiguous prefix at the first boundary.
604	The d0/d1 boundary, near-perfect at 1--1.5B (AUC ${>}0.99$), attenuates to 0.56--0.64 at 14B.
605	
606	Each strategy produces a structurally distinct adjacent-pair AUC pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}):
607	nucleus is monotonically increasing (d0v1=0.643 $<$ d1v2=0.713 $<$ d2v3=0.728);
608	temperature exhibits d2v3 collapse (0.618, the weakest boundary) because temperature scaling uniformly amplifies logit differences, accelerating convergence to the attractor distribution at deeper depths;
609	top-$k$ exhibits d0v1 collapse (0.561, the weakest boundary) because vocabulary truncation constrains d0 and d1 distributions to the same top-$k$ tokens, compressing their statistical distance.
610	This three-way divergence confirms at 14B that different decoding strategies induce structurally distinct degradation trajectories (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
611	
612	Feature importance shifts under top-$k$: lexical diversity metrics (TTR, hapax ratio) enter the top-5, displacing perplexity tail statistics that dominate under nucleus and temperature.
613	This result carries an important caveat: Qwen2.5-14B uses self-reference, whereas all other experiments use Pythia-1.4B.
614	Self-reference conflates the generator's distributional shift with the reference model's sensitivity; the observed attenuation may partially reflect the reference model change rather than a pure scale effect.
615	
616	\paragraph{(5) Cross-reference control at 14B.}
617	To disentangle the scale effect from the reference-model artifact, we re-extract all 19 features using Pythia-1.4B (the same reference model as the 1--1.5B benchmark) on the Qwen2.5-14B generated text.
618	Table~\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b} compares self-reference and cross-reference results per strategy.
619	
620	\begin{table}[h]
621	\centering
622	\small
623	\caption{14B cross-reference control: Pythia-1.4B reference vs.\ self-reference on Qwen2.5-14B text. $\Delta$: cross $-$ self. $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ computed from consecutive pairwise AUC.}
624	\label{tab:cross_ref_14b}
625	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
626	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
627	\toprule
628	Strategy & Self-ref (\%) & Cross-ref (\%) & $\Delta$ & $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\
629	\midrule
630	nucleus & 54.1 & 45.0 & $-$9.1 & 2 & 0.702 \\
631	temp ($\tau{=}0.9$) & 50.5 & 46.3 & $-$4.2 & 2 & 0.739 \\
632	top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 47.9 & 40.9 & $-$7.1 & 0 & 0.699 \\
633	\cmidrule{2-6}
634	\textit{mean} & \textit{50.8} & \textit{44.1} & $-$\textit{6.8} & --- & --- \\
635	\bottomrule
636	\end{tabular}}
637	\end{table}
638	
639	Cross-reference accuracy drops by 6.8\,pp on average (range 4.2--9.1\,pp), but $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ \emph{rises} from 0 to 2 for nucleus and temperature: the consecutive d0v1 (0.70--0.74) and d1v2 (0.72--0.73) AUCs now exceed the 0.70 threshold, while d2v3 remains below (0.54--0.61).
640	Under self-reference, the first boundary d0v1 falls below $T{=}0.70$ in all strategies (0.56--0.64), breaking the contiguous prefix and yielding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$ even though some later pairs (e.g., nucleus d1v2\,=\,0.713, d2v3\,=\,0.728) exceed the threshold; the Pythia-1.4B reference model provides sharper distributional contrast at the cost of overall accuracy.
641	The ${\sim}$7\,pp accuracy gap between self- and cross-reference is therefore a reference-model artifact, not a depth-signal artifact: depth signal persists at 14B albeit attenuated relative to 1--1.5B.
642	
643	\paragraph{Caveats.}
644	For Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\%) does not exclude sub-60\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\% range at 1--1.5B.
645	The divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.
646	
647	\paragraph{Confounds.}
648	\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}
649	Four confounds limit the 7--8B results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling at 7--8B (the 14B model addresses this with all three strategies); (4)~vocabulary mismatch between larger generators and the 1.4B reference model.
650	For the 14B model specifically: the cross-reference control (Table~\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b}) quantifies the reference-model artifact at ${\sim}$7\,pp, but the remaining accuracy gap (44\% vs.\ 78--85\% at 1--1.5B) still conflates scale with the other confounds listed above.
651	The base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.
652	The remaining confounds are not mutually independent (quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution); it is therefore infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\% vs.\ 78--85\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.
653	
654	\subsection{Qwen2.5-7B Self-Reference Scale Probe}
655	\label{app:qwen7b_selfref}
656	
657	The preceding 7--8B experiments (\S\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) use 4-bit NF4 QLoRA with Pythia-1.4B cross-reference, leaving two confounds unresolved: (1)~quantization-induced perplexity distortion and (2)~vocabulary mismatch between the 7B generator and the 1.4B reference model.
658	To isolate the scale effect from these artifacts, we run the full recursive generation pipeline on Qwen2.5-7B \cite{qwen2.5} (base model, not instruction-tuned) using FP16 LoRA fine-tuning and self-reference feature extraction (the same model serves as both generator and reference).
659	
660	\paragraph{Setup.}
661	At each depth $k \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, a fresh LoRA adapter ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, target modules \texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{k\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}) is initialized on the base model and trained on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ for 3 epochs (lr $2{\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch size 16 via $4 \times 4$ gradient accumulation, bfloat16 mixed precision), following the same protocol as the 1--1.5B benchmark (\S\ref{sec:method:benchmark}).
662	Generation uses nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), producing 5{,}000 passages per depth (20{,}000 total, matching the per-cell sample size of the primary benchmark).
663	Feature extraction computes the standard 19-dimensional feature vector $\mathbf{f}(x)$ using the model's own token-level log-probabilities as the reference distribution, eliminating the cross-model reference artifact quantified in Table~\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b}.
664	Classification uses the same 5-fold stratified Random Forest (200 trees, seed 42) as all other cells.
665	
666	\paragraph{Comparison to prior 7B experiments.}
667	This experiment differs from the QLoRA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct entry in Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale} in three respects: (i)~FP16 LoRA removes quantization distortion, (ii)~self-reference removes vocabulary mismatch, and (iii)~5{,}000 samples per depth (vs.\ 500) narrows confidence intervals.
668	If depth signal persists under these cleaner conditions, the attenuation observed in the QLoRA experiments reflects genuine scale effects rather than methodological artifacts.
669	
670	\paragraph{Results.}
671	The self-reference probe achieves 52.1\% four-class accuracy (CI [51.4, 52.7], $2.1{\times}$ chance, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$; $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.65}{=}3$).
672	Pairwise AUC: d0-vs-d1 = 0.697, d1-vs-d2 = 0.673, d2-vs-d3 = 0.659; top-5 features: \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{skew\_ppl}, \texttt{p90\_surprisal}, \texttt{kurt\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}.
673	Contrary to the hypothesis that removing quantization and vocabulary-mismatch confounds would improve accuracy, self-reference at 7B is 8\,pp below the QLoRA cross-reference baseline (60.1\%), and closely matches the 14B self-reference result (50.8\%, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$).
674	This reveals a self-reference ceiling: larger models assign similar probabilities to d0 and d1 text, collapsing d0v1 discriminability (AUC\,=\,0.697 vs.\ 0.969 under cross-reference).
675	Cross-model reference strategies, where a smaller observer model provides complementary distributional perspective, may be necessary for effective depth estimation above ${\sim}$7B.
676	
677	\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}
678	\label{app:cross_boundary}
679	
680	The preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.
681	In practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.
682	We probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.
683	
684	\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}
685	We construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.
686	Each recursive step uses a \emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.
687	A 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\% accuracy (95\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).
688	Pairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.
689	These results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries. This constitutes preliminary evidence for a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.
690	
691	\paragraph{Confound discussion.}
692	The heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).
693	However, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.
694	The observed 84.75\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.
695	We address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.
696	
697	\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}
698	To empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\to$ OLMo $\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.
699	The reverse chain achieves 85.75\% (95\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\%, [81.0, 88.25]).
700	If the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.
701	
702	\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}
703	All prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.
704	To test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).
705	Five-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\% $\pm$ 2.0\% accuracy, compared to ${\sim}$78\% on the WikiText seed.
706	Per-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66, with d2 remaining the most challenging class.
707	The 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.
708	These results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.
709	
710	\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}
711	\label{app:seed_replication}
712	
713	Table~\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed accuracy for all three model families (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.
714	
715	\begin{table}[h]
716	\centering
717	\small
718	\caption{Cross-seed replication (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d3). seed=42 is the primary benchmark (matching Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix}); seed=43 is an independent replication.}
719	\label{tab:seed_replication}
720	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
721	\toprule
722	Model & Seed & Acc (\%) & 95\% CI & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
723	\midrule
724	Pythia-1.4B & 42 & 78.70 & [78.10, 79.24] & --- \\
725	Pythia-1.4B & 43 & 78.84 & [78.13, 79.52] & $+$0.14 \\
726	\midrule
727	OLMo-1B & 42 & 78.94 & [78.38, 79.48] & --- \\
728	OLMo-1B & 43 & 82.11 & [81.77, 82.54] & $+$3.17 \\
729	\midrule
730	GPT-2~XL & 42 & 84.28 & [83.78, 84.80] & --- \\
731	GPT-2~XL & 43 & 86.32 & [86.17, 86.49] & $+$2.04 \\
732	\bottomrule
733	\end{tabular}
734	\end{table}
735	
736	Seed=43 yields higher accuracy in all three models ($+$0.14 to $+$3.17\,pp, same direction).
737	For Pythia ($+$0.14\,pp), CIs overlap, consistent with noise; for OLMo ($+$3.17\,pp) and GPT-2~XL ($+$2.04\,pp), CIs do not overlap, so LoRA initialization has a non-trivial effect on absolute accuracy (${\sim}$1--3\,pp).
738	Top-3 features are identical across all seeds and models (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}); depth ordering is preserved in every configuration.
739	
740	\paragraph{Outlier handling.}
741	Feature extraction occasionally produces extreme perplexity values (outlier passages); we apply per-feature clipping at $\pm 10^{10}$ before classification, affecting ${<}0.1$\% of samples.
742	The d3 perplexity distribution varies substantially across seeds: under seed=42, 0.56\% of d3 passages exceed $10^6$ in raw perplexity, while under seed=43 this fraction rises to 12.8\%.
743	Despite this variation, the classifier remains effective after clipping, confirming that the depth signal resides in the p90-and-below distributional shape rather than extreme outliers.
744	
745	\subsection{Depth-4 Extension}
746	\label{app:depth4_ext}
747	
748	We extend all three model families (GPT-2~XL, Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B; nucleus $p{=}0.95$) to depth~4 and train 5-class RF classifiers (d0--d4, 5{,}000 samples per depth).
749	Five-class accuracy reaches 80.5\% (OLMo), 74.0\% (GPT-2~XL), and 71.3\% (Pythia), all substantially above the 20\% chance baseline.
750	For Pythia and GPT-2~XL, d3 recall drops from ${\sim}$80\% (4-class) to ${\sim}$49\% (5-class) due to d3/d4 confusion; d4 recall is higher (Pythia 68.1\%, GPT-2~XL 63.9\%).
751	$\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \geq 4$ was established by dedicated binary classifiers in the atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}; d3v4 AUC: Pythia 0.784, OLMo 0.765, GPT-2~XL 0.750); the 5-class results provide complementary evidence that depth~4 remains discriminable in the multi-class setting.
752	
753	\paragraph{Feature monotonicity at d4.}
754	For Pythia and GPT-2~XL, the dominant depth markers (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}) maintain monotonic decrease through d4 (Pythia \texttt{p90\_ppl}: $483.7 \to 12.5$ from d0 to d4; \texttt{var\_surprisal}: $6.50 \to 1.29$), consistent with continued tail erosion (\S\ref{sec:method:theory}).
755	Mean perplexity breaks monotonicity at d4 for GPT-2~XL (d3: $1{,}076\mathrm{K}$ $\to$ d4: $928\mathrm{K}$) while tail perplexity continues to decline.
756	OLMo exhibits a \texttt{var\_surprisal} rebound at d4 ($2.49 \to 2.83$, non-monotonic), creating a feature signature absent in GPT-2~XL and Pythia.
757	The 5-class RF exploits non-adjacent depth contrasts (particularly d0-vs-d4 and d2-vs-d4) to achieve 80.5\% accuracy, the highest among all three families, while the dedicated binary d3-vs-d4 classifier (AUC 0.765) is restricted to adjacent-depth features.
758	This discrepancy illustrates that multi-class classifiers can exploit global distributional structure unavailable to pairwise comparisons.
759	The divergence between aggregate and tail statistics suggests that aggregate distributional statistics saturate at deep recursion while tail statistics continue to shift.
760	This saturation provides a physical mechanism for the EDD ceiling: once per-step feature change falls below classifier resolution, adjacent depths become indistinguishable regardless of continued distributional drift.
761	
762	\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}
763	\label{app:length_ablation}
764	
765	Table~\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).
766	
767	\begin{table}[h]
768	\centering
769	\small
770	\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}
771	\label{tab:length_ablation}
772	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
773	\toprule
774	Passage length (tokens) & Acc (\%) \\
775	\midrule
776	128 & 68.35 \\
777	256 & 73.80 \\
778	512 & 77.82 \\
779	\bottomrule
780	\end{tabular}
781	\end{table}
782	
783	Depth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.
784	The monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.
785	
786	\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}
787	\label{app:mixed_depth}
788	
789	Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\,pp tolerance).
790	Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\,pp to $-$2.43\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus, as Random Forest produces identical per-sample predictions regardless of batch context.
791	
792	\subsection{Learned Estimator Comparison}
793	\label{app:learned_estimator}
794	
795	Table~\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss \cite{coral2020}, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}.
796	The ordinal MLP follows the nested binary reduction framework \cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal,frank2001} with the loss function of \citet{rennie2005}.
797	All three methods operate on the same 19-dimensional feature vector; the comparison isolates the effect of the classification head.
798

case (0.56--0.64), breaking the contiguous prefix at the first boundary.\nThe d0/d1 boundary, near-perfect at 1--1.5B (AUC ${>}0.99$), attenuates to 0.56--0.64 at 14B.\n\nEach strategy produces a structurally distinct adjacent-pair AUC pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}):\nnucleus is monotonically increasing (d0v1=0.643 $<$ d1v2=0.713 $<$ d2v3=0.728);\ntemperature exhibits d2v3 collapse (0.618, the weakest boundary) because temperature scaling uniformly amplifies logit differences, accelerating convergence to the attractor distribution at deeper depths;\ntop-$k$ exhibits d0v1 collapse (0.561, the weakest boundary) because vocabulary truncation constrains d0 and d1 distributions to the same top-$k$ tokens, compressing their statistical distance.\nThis three-way divergence confirms at 14B that different decoding strategies induce structurally distinct degradation trajectories (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\n\nFeature importance shifts under top-$k$: lexical diversity metrics (TTR, hapax ratio) enter the top-5, displacing perplexity tail statistics that dominate under nucleus and temperature.\nThis result carries an important caveat: Qwen2.5-14B uses self-reference, whereas all other experiments use Pythia-1.4B.\nSelf-reference conflates the generator's distributional shift with the reference model's sensitivity; the observed attenuation may partially reflect the reference model change rather than a pure scale effect.\n\n\\paragraph{(5) Cross-reference control at 14B.}\nTo disentangle the scale effect from the reference-model artifact, we re-extract all 19 features using Pythia-1.4B (the same reference model as the 1--1.5B benchmark) on the Qwen2.5-14B generated text.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b} compares self-reference and cross-reference results per strategy.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{14B cross-reference control: Pythia-1.4B reference vs.\\ self-reference on Qwen2.5-14B text. $\\Delta$: cross $-$ self. $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ computed from consecutive pairwise AUC.}\n\\label{tab:cross_ref_14b}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Self-ref (\\%) & Cross-ref (\\%) & $\\Delta$ & $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & 54.1 & 45.0 & $-$9.1 & 2 & 0.702 \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 50.5 & 46.3 & $-$4.2 & 2 & 0.739 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 47.9 & 40.9 & $-$7.1 & 0 & 0.699 \\\\\n\\cmidrule{2-6}\n\\textit{mean} & \\textit{50.8} & \\textit{44.1} & $-$\\textit{6.8} & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nCross-reference accuracy drops by 6.8\\,pp on average (range 4.2--9.1\\,pp), but $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ \\emph{rises} from 0 to 2 for nucleus and temperature: the consecutive d0v1 (0.70--0.74) and d1v2 (0.72--0.73) AUCs now exceed the 0.70 threshold, while d2v3 remains below (0.54--0.61).\nUnder self-reference, the first boundary d0v1 falls below $T{=}0.70$ in all strategies (0.56--0.64), breaking the contiguous prefix and yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$ even though some later pairs (e.g., nucleus d1v2\\,=\\,0.713, d2v3\\,=\\,0.728) exceed the threshold; the Pythia-1.4B reference model provides sharper distributional contrast at the cost of overall accuracy.\nThe ${\\sim}$7\\,pp accuracy gap between self- and cross-reference is therefore a reference-model artifact, not a depth-signal artifact: depth signal persists at 14B albeit attenuated relative to 1--1.5B.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.}\nFor Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\\%) does not exclude sub-60\\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\\% range at 1--1.5B.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds.}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}\nFour confounds limit the 7--8B results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling at 7--8B (the 14B model addresses this with all three strategies); (4)~vocabulary mismatch between larger generators and the 1.4B reference model.\nFor the 14B model specifically: the cross-reference control (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b}) quantifies the reference-model artifact at ${\\sim}$7\\,pp, but the remaining accuracy gap (44\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) still conflates scale with the other confounds listed above.\nThe base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.\nThe remaining confounds are not mutually independent (quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution); it is therefore infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.\n\n\\subsection{Qwen2.5-7B Self-Reference Scale Probe}\n\\label{app:qwen7b_selfref}\n\nThe preceding 7--8B experiments (\\S\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) use 4-bit NF4 QLoRA with Pythia-1.4B cross-reference, leaving two confounds unresolved: (1)~quantization-induced perplexity distortion and (2)~vocabulary mismatch between the 7B generator and the 1.4B reference model.\nTo isolate the scale effect from these artifacts, we run the full recursive generation pipeline on Qwen2.5-7B \\cite{qwen2.5} (base model, not instruction-tuned) using FP16 LoRA fine-tuning and self-reference feature extraction (the same model serves as both generator and reference).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nAt each depth $k \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, a fresh LoRA adapter ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, target modules \\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{k\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}) is initialized on the base model and trained on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ for 3 epochs (lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch size 16 via $4 \\times 4$ gradient accumulation, bfloat16 mixed precision), following the same protocol as the 1--1.5B benchmark (\\S\\ref{sec:method:benchmark}).\nGeneration uses nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$), producing 5{,}000 passages per depth (20{,}000 total, matching the per-cell sample size of the primary benchmark).\nFeature extraction computes the standard 19-dimensional feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ using the model's own token-level log-probabilities as the reference distribution, eliminating the cross-model reference artifact quantified in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_ref_14b}.\nClassification uses the same 5-fold stratified Random Forest (200 trees, seed 42) as all other cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to prior 7B experiments.}\nThis experiment differs from the QLoRA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct entry in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} in three respects: (i)~FP16 LoRA removes quantization distortion, (ii)~self-reference removes vocabulary mismatch, and (iii)~5{,}000 samples per depth (vs.\\ 500) narrows confidence intervals.\nIf depth signal persists under these cleaner conditions, the attenuation observed in the QLoRA experiments reflects genuine scale effects rather than methodological artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nThe self-reference probe achieves 52.1\\% four-class accuracy (CI [51.4, 52.7], $2.1{\\times}$ chance, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.65}{=}3$).\nPairwise AUC: d0-vs-d1 = 0.697, d1-vs-d2 = 0.673, d2-vs-d3 = 0.659; top-5 features: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{skew\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p90\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{kurt\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}.\nContrary to the hypothesis that removing quantization and vocabulary-mismatch confounds would improve accuracy, self-reference at 7B is 8\\,pp below the QLoRA cross-reference baseline (60.1\\%), and closely matches the 14B self-reference result (50.8\\%, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$).\nThis reveals a self-reference ceiling: larger models assign similar probabilities to d0 and d1 text, collapsing d0v1 discriminability (AUC\\,=\\,0.697 vs.\\ 0.969 under cross-reference).\nCross-model reference strategies, where a smaller observer model provides complementary distributional perspective, may be necessary for effective depth estimation above ${\\sim}$7B.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries. This constitutes preliminary evidence for a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nWe address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.\n\n\\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}\nTo empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\\to$ OLMo $\\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.\nThe reverse chain achieves 85.75\\% (95\\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\\%, [81.0, 88.25]).\nIf the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66, with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}\n\\label{app:seed_replication}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed accuracy for all three model families (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-seed replication (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d3). seed=42 is the primary benchmark (matching Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix}); seed=43 is an independent replication.}\n\\label{tab:seed_replication}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Seed & Acc (\\%) & 95\\% CI & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & 42 & 78.70 & [78.10, 79.24] & --- \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 43 & 78.84 & [78.13, 79.52] & $+$0.14 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-1B & 42 & 78.94 & [78.38, 79.48] & --- \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 43 & 82.11 & [81.77, 82.54] & $+$3.17 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL & 42 & 84.28 & [83.78, 84.80] & --- \\\\\nGPT-2~XL & 43 & 86.32 & [86.17, 86.49] & $+$2.04 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed=43 yields higher accuracy in all three models ($+$0.14 to $+$3.17\\,pp, same direction).\nFor Pythia ($+$0.14\\,pp), CIs overlap, consistent with noise; for OLMo ($+$3.17\\,pp) and GPT-2~XL ($+$2.04\\,pp), CIs do not overlap, so LoRA initialization has a non-trivial effect on absolute accuracy (${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp).\nTop-3 features are identical across all seeds and models (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}); depth ordering is preserved in every configuration.\n\n\\paragraph{Outlier handling.}\nFeature extraction occasionally produces extreme perplexity values (outlier passages); we apply per-feature clipping at $\\pm 10^{10}$ before classification, affecting ${<}0.1$\\% of samples.\nThe d3 perplexity distribution varies substantially across seeds: under seed=42, 0.56\\% of d3 passages exceed $10^6$ in raw perplexity, while under seed=43 this fraction rises to 12.8\\%.\nDespite this variation, the classifier remains effective after clipping, confirming that the depth signal resides in the p90-and-below distributional shape rather than extreme outliers.\n\n\\subsection{Depth-4 Extension}\n\\label{app:depth4_ext}\n\nWe extend all three model families (GPT-2~XL, Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B; nucleus $p{=}0.95$) to depth~4 and train 5-class RF classifiers (d0--d4, 5{,}000 samples per depth).\nFive-class accuracy reaches 80.5\\% (OLMo), 74.0\\% (GPT-2~XL), and 71.3\\% (Pythia), all substantially above the 20\\% chance baseline.\nFor Pythia and GPT-2~XL, d3 recall drops from ${\\sim}$80\\% (4-class) to ${\\sim}$49\\% (5-class) due to d3/d4 confusion; d4 recall is higher (Pythia 68.1\\%, GPT-2~XL 63.9\\%).\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 4$ was established by dedicated binary classifiers in the atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}; d3v4 AUC: Pythia 0.784, OLMo 0.765, GPT-2~XL 0.750); the 5-class results provide complementary evidence that depth~4 remains discriminable in the multi-class setting.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature monotonicity at d4.}\nFor Pythia and GPT-2~XL, the dominant depth markers (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) maintain monotonic decrease through d4 (Pythia \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}: $483.7 \\to 12.5$ from d0 to d4; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}: $6.50 \\to 1.29$), consistent with continued tail erosion (\\S\\ref{sec:method:theory}).\nMean perplexity breaks monotonicity at d4 for GPT-2~XL (d3: $1{,}076\\mathrm{K}$ $\\to$ d4: $928\\mathrm{K}$) while tail perplexity continues to decline.\nOLMo exhibits a \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} rebound at d4 ($2.49 \\to 2.83$, non-monotonic), creating a feature signature absent in GPT-2~XL and Pythia.\nThe 5-class RF exploits non-adjacent depth contrasts (particularly d0-vs-d4 and d2-vs-d4) to achieve 80.5\\% accuracy, the highest among all three families, while the dedicated binary d3-vs-d4 classifier (AUC 0.765) is restricted to adjacent-depth features.\nThis discrepancy illustrates that multi-class classifiers can exploit global distributional structure unavailable to pairwise comparisons.\nThe divergence between aggregate and tail statistics suggests that aggregate distributional statistics saturate at deep recursion while tail statistics continue to shift.\nThis saturation provides a physical mechanism for the EDD ceiling: once per-step feature change falls below classifier resolution, adjacent depths become indistinguishable regardless of continued distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}\n\\label{app:length_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}\n\\label{tab:length_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPassage length (tokens) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n128 & 68.35 \\\\\n256 & 73.80 \\\\\n512 & 77.82 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDepth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.\nThe monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.\n\n\\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}\n\\label{app:mixed_depth}\n\nPer-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\\,pp tolerance).\nIndividual class deltas range from $-$0.45\\,pp to $-$2.43\\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus, as Random Forest produces identical per-sample predictions regardless of batch context.\n\n\\subsection{Learned Estimator Comparison}\n\\label{app:learned_estimator}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss \\cite{coral2020}, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}.\nThe ordinal MLP follows the nested binary reduction framework \\cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal,frank2001} with the loss function of \\citet{rennie2005}.\nAll three methods operate on the same 19-dimensional feature vector; the comparison isolates the effect of the classification head.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]",

    }

819	\subsection{Instruction-Tuned Model at 1.5B Scale}
820	\label{app:instruct_1p5b}
821	
822	To test whether instruction tuning eliminates depth signal at matched scale (1.5B, avoiding the quantization confound of \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), we run the full recursive pipeline on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with self-reference feature extraction (i.e., the same model serves as both generator and reference).
823	
824	The RF classifier achieves 47.4\% four-class accuracy (95\% CI $[46.7, 48.1]$, $1.9\times$ chance); instruction tuning therefore reduces but does not eliminate depth signal.
825	The pairwise AUC profile differs from base models: d0v1 = 0.653, d1v2 = 0.620, d2v3 = 0.664, with a gap-dependent pattern (non-adjacent pairs yield higher AUC, e.g., d0v3 = 0.902) but no pair reaching the 0.75 threshold.
826	
827	The feature importance ranking shifts: the perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}) that dominate in base models drop in relative importance, while surprisal variance and mean-level features gain rank.
828	This shift suggests instruction tuning compresses the perplexity distribution (reducing tail discriminability) while preserving lower-order statistical patterns.
829	
830	Two caveats apply: (1) self-reference evaluation may inflate or deflate accuracy relative to cross-reference evaluation (cf.\ \S\ref{app:ref_ablation}); (2) a single instruction-tuned model does not establish the generality of the feature shift across architectures or instruction-tuning recipes.

853	\end{table}
854	
855	The hypothesis that d0-only data yields the lowest perplexity is not supported: d1-only and mixed subsets tie for best (PPL~5.55), both marginally below d0-only (5.65).
856	The PPL spread is small (0.12, 2.1\% relative) and the sample size (500 per condition, single seed) is insufficient for statistical significance.
857	Training loss decreases monotonically with depth (d0: 2.558, d1: 1.920, d2: 1.286), consistent with recursive generation reducing information content and making text easier to model.
858	This pilot establishes that depth-filtered data is not harmful for downstream fine-tuning; whether depth-aware curation provides a meaningful advantage requires larger-scale evaluation.
859

931	
932	\subsection{Binary Detector Baselines for Ordinal Classification}
933	\label{app:detector_baselines}
934	
935	We evaluate whether existing binary AI-text detectors and membership inference methods, when repurposed for ordinal classification, can match our 19-feature RF pipeline.
936	
937	\paragraph{Binary detectors (Pythia nucleus).}
938	Binoculars~\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.7\%).
939	The 4.5\,pp gap (74.2\% vs.\ 78.7\%) confirms that perplexity distributional features capture ordinal depth structure beyond what generic detection scores provide.
940	Note that Fast-DetectGPT exhibits numerical overflow on Pythia nucleus d1 (mean perturbation score $>$28{,}000); results for this cell should be interpreted cautiously.
941	Across all 9 atlas cells, Binoculars and Fast-DetectGPT ordinal accuracy averages ${\sim}$35\% and ${\sim}$32\%, respectively.
942	
943	\paragraph{Membership inference baselines (all 9 cells).}
944	DetectGPT~\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} (gpt2-large scoring model, t5-large perturbation model, 5 perturbations) achieves 30.7\% mean 4-class accuracy (d1--d3 BalAcc: 24.3\%); Table~\ref{tab:baselines} reports 28.8\% under the best-of-3 calibration protocol, which selects the optimal threshold configuration per cell.
945	Min-K\% Prob~\cite{shi2024_minkprob} ($K{=}20$) with CORAL ordinal calibration reaches 33.2\% (d1--d3 BalAcc: 40.4\%); the best single cell is gpt2xl\_temp09 at 50.6\%.
946	Min-K\%'s advantage over DetectGPT (+2.5\,pp) is consistent with token-level log-probabilities containing more depth-relevant information than perturbation-based curvature, but both remain within 10\,pp of chance.
947	CORAL calibration improves ordinal structure (d1--d3 BalAcc 40.4\% vs.\ 33.3\% random) without closing the gap to RF (71.6\%), confirming that the bottleneck is the input signal rather than the calibration strategy.
948	Perturbation-based and membership-inference methods (28--33\%) confirm that token-level detection scores lack ordinal depth resolution.
949	Cross-perplexity (Binoculars) is a notable exception: when evaluated across all 9 atlas cells with threshold calibration, Binoculars achieves 69.8\%; with multi-feature RF calibration (11 cross-perplexity statistics), it reaches 78.1\%, matching our 19-feature pipeline within 0.3\,pp (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}).
950	
951	\paragraph{Augmented feature set.}
952	To test whether detection scores carry complementary ordinal signal, we add Min-K\% probability ($K{=}20$) and DetectGPT perturbation discrepancy as additional RF features.
953	The 21-feature model (19 + Min-K\% + DetectGPT) achieves 78.90\% mean 4-class accuracy (+0.48\,pp) and 72.20\% d1--d3 balanced accuracy (+0.60\,pp).
954	Min-K\% ranks \#3 by feature importance (0.095), behind \texttt{p90\_ppl} (0.161) and \texttt{var\_surprisal} (0.126); DetectGPT ranks \#17 (0.026).
955	The marginal improvement is consistent across 8 of 9 atlas cells, indicating that Min-K\%'s token-level log-probability signal provides weak but real complementary information to the passage-level distributional features.
956	The top-2 features (\texttt{p90\_ppl} + \texttt{var\_surprisal}) retain combined importance of 28.7\%, confirming that perplexity tail statistics remain the dominant depth markers even after augmentation.
957	
958	\paragraph{Pairwise AUC and ordinal collapse.}
959	Table~\ref{tab:detector_pairwise_auc} reports pairwise binary AUC for three nucleus cells, contrasting our 19-feature RF with LR-calibrated detector scores and their combination.
960	Detectors achieve reasonable d0-vs-d1 separation (Combined AUC\,$\geq$\,0.949), but d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 AUC collapse toward chance (0.50--0.77), far below the RF pipeline (0.930--0.958 and 0.813--0.892 respectively).
961	This confirms that binary detection scores capture the real-vs-synthetic boundary but lack the ordinal gradient needed to distinguish recursive depths.

991	\subsection{Binoculars Feature Ablation and Signal Decomposition}
992	\label{app:feature_ablation_bino}
993	
994	To quantify the overlap and complementarity between our 19-feature pipeline and Binoculars' cross-perplexity features, we evaluate four feature configurations across all 9 atlas cells (Table~\ref{tab:bino_ablation}).
995	
996	\begin{table}[h]
997	\centering
998	\small
999	\caption{Feature ablation: overlap and merged configurations (9-cell mean, 5-fold CV, RF 200 trees). The 6 overlap features are the perplexity statistics shared between both pipelines (mean, variance, median, p10, p90, skewness).}
1000	\label{tab:bino_ablation}
1001	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
1002	\toprule
1003	Configuration & \#Feat. & Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ vs.\ 19-feat \\
1004	\midrule
1005	6 overlap only & 6 & 75.9 & $-$2.5 \\
1006	Our 19 features & 19 & 78.4 & --- \\
1007	Binoculars 11 features & 11 & 78.1 & $-$0.3 \\

1009	\bottomrule
1010	\end{tabular}
1011	\end{table}
1012	
1013	The 6 shared perplexity features alone achieve 75.9\% (97\% of the full pipeline's accuracy), confirming that mean-level perplexity distributional displacement is the core depth signal.
1014	Our 13 additional features (kurtosis, surprisal variance, lexical/n-gram statistics) contribute +2.5\,pp of shape-level information; Binoculars' 5 unique features (observer-performer agreement score, cross-entropy statistics) contribute +4.2\,pp of cross-model divergence when merged.
1015

Bino-11 per-cell values omitted because Binoculars features were computed on the pooled 9-cell corpus; the per-cell mean (78.1\%) is reported in Table~\ref{tab:bino_ablation}.}
1022	\label{tab:bino_ablation_percell}
1023	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

1032	OLMo temp09 & 81.0 & 82.3 & --- & 88.3 & +6.0 \\
1033	OLMo top-$k$50 & 72.5 & 74.4 & --- & 78.4 & +4.0 \\

1035	GPT-2~XL temp09 & 84.0 & 85.9 & --- & 88.5 & +2.6 \\
1036	GPT-2~XL top-$k$50 & 72.1 & 75.8 & --- & 79.1 & +3.3 \\
1037	\midrule
1038	\textbf{9-cell mean} & \textbf{75.9} & \textbf{78.4} & \textbf{78.1} & \textbf{82.6} & \textbf{+4.2} \\

1041	\end{table}
1042	
1043	\paragraph{Strategy-dependent complementarity.}
1044	The merged improvement varies by decoding strategy: nucleus and temperature cells gain +4.7\,pp on average, while top-k cells gain +3.2\,pp.
1045	Top-k sampling truncates the token distribution to the $k$ highest-probability tokens, producing text with reduced distributional diversity; this limits the information available to cross-model divergence features, which rely on disagreement between observer and performer predictions.
1046	Merged improvement also varies by generator family: OLMo cells (observer-dissimilar) gain +5.1\,pp on average, compared to +3.9\,pp for Pythia (observer-similar) and +3.5\,pp for GPT-2~XL, suggesting cross-model divergence features contribute more when observer and generator architectures differ.
1047	
1048	\paragraph{Cross-scale Binoculars ablation (14B).}
1049	We evaluate Binoculars cross-scale on two representative strategies (temperature, top-$k$); the nucleus cell was used for the main 14B pipeline and its GPU hours were allocated to generation rather than Binoculars evaluation.
1050	At 14B, when only 1--1.5B reference models are available for cross-perplexity computation (Pythia-1.4B observer analyzing Qwen2.5-14B text), Binoculars features degrade substantially (Table~\ref{tab:bino_14b_ablation}).
1051	Self-reference statistical features (19-feat) outperform Binoculars (11-feat) by 8--11\,pp, reversing the near-parity observed at 1--1.5B.
1052	Merging all 30 features yields a consistent +3.6--3.7\,pp gain over the self-reference baseline across both tested strategies, confirming that cross-perplexity features retain complementary signal even under the 10$\times$ scale gap, though the gain is attenuated relative to +4.2\,pp at 1--1.5B.
1053	
1054	\begin{table}[h]
1055	\centering
1056	\small
1057	\caption{Feature ablation at 14B (Qwen2.5-14B, self-reference$^\dagger$). Binoculars: observer=Pythia-1.4B, performer=Pythia-410M. $\Delta$: merged minus main-pipeline stat-only accuracy (Method~A alignment). Chance = 25\%.}
1058	\label{tab:bino_14b_ablation}
1059	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
1060	\toprule
1061	Strategy & Bino-11 & Stat-19$^\dagger$ & Merged-30 & $\Delta$ \\
1062	\midrule
1063	temp09 & 42.6 & 50.5 & 54.2 & +3.7 \\
1064	top-$k$50 & 37.7 & 47.9 & 51.5 & +3.6 \\
1065	\bottomrule
1066	\end{tabular}
1067	\end{table}
1068	
1069	The Binoculars degradation reflects a practical deployment constraint: the 10$\times$ scale gap between observer (1.4B) and generator (14B) causes cross-perplexity features to lose sensitivity, not a fundamental limitation of the cross-model divergence channel.
1070	Self-reference features are more robust across scales because they avoid the observer--generator mismatch.
1071	The accuracy difference between self-reference (50.8\%) and Binoculars stat-only (40.1\% mean) may therefore partially reflect observer capacity limitations rather than feature design differences.

Groups merge the four Table~\\ref{tab:features} categories into three: bigram/trigram entropy move from Lexical Diversity to Entropy/Surprisal; TTR/hapax move to Repetition/Diversity. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:feature_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nFeature group & \\#Feat. & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ vs.\\ Full \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (all 19) & 19 & 66.4 & --- \\\\\nPerplexity only & 9 & 66.0 & $-$0.4 \\\\\nEntropy/Surprisal only & 5 & 63.2 & $-$3.2 \\\\\nRepetition/Diversity only & 5 & 53.0 & $-$13.4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} only & 1 & 58.3 & $-$8.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder both settings perplexity features nearly match the full set, while repetition/diversity features fall far below; perplexity degradation statistics carry the dominant depth signal.\nThe top-2 individual features by Gini importance are \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (22.4\\%) and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (18.8\\%), accounting for 41\\% of total importance.\n\n\\subsection{RF Seed Robustness}\n\\label{app:rf_seed}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:rf_seed} reports cross-model transfer accuracy across three RF random seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{RF seed robustness (cross-model LOO, nucleus, 4-class).}\n\\label{tab:rf_seed}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nSeed & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n42 & 66.4 \\\\\n123 & 66.5 \\\\\n456 & 66.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean $\\pm$ std & 66.48 $\\pm$ 0.06 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 0.06\\,pp standard deviation across seeds confirms that RF classification is deterministic to within rounding noise at this sample size, and reported accuracy differences across experimental conditions (e.g., feature ablation, cross-model transfer) are not attributable to classifier randomness.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Detector Baselines for Ordinal Classification}\n\\label{app:detector_baselines}\n\nWe evaluate whether existing binary AI-text detectors and membership inference methods, when repurposed for ordinal classification, can match our 19-feature RF pipeline.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary detectors (Pythia nucleus).}\nBinoculars~\\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.7\\%).\nThe 4.5\\,pp gap (74.2\\% vs.\\ 78.7\\%) confirms that perplexity distributional features capture ordinal depth structure beyond what generic detection scores provide.\nNote that Fast-DetectGPT exhibits numerical overflow on Pythia nucleus d1 (mean perturbation score $>$28{,}000); results for this cell should be interpreted cautiously.\nAcross all 9 atlas cells, Binoculars and Fast-DetectGPT ordinal accuracy averages ${\\sim}$35\\% and ${\\sim}$32\\%, respectively.\n\n\\paragraph{Membership inference baselines (all 9 cells).}\nDetectGPT~\\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} (gpt2-large scoring model, t5-large perturbation model, 5 perturbations) achieves 30.7\\% mean 4-class accuracy (d1--d3 BalAcc: 24.3\\%); Table~\\ref{tab:baselines} reports 28.8\\% under the best-of-3 calibration protocol, which selects the optimal threshold configuration per cell.\nMin-K\\% Prob~\\cite{shi2024_minkprob} ($K{=}20$) with CORAL ordinal calibration reaches 33.2\\% (d1--d3 BalAcc: 40.4\\%); the best single cell is gpt2xl\\_temp09 at 50.6\\%.\nMin-K\\%'s advantage over DetectGPT (+2.5\\,pp) is consistent with token-level log-probabilities containing more depth-relevant information than perturbation-based curvature, but both remain within 10\\,pp of chance.\nCORAL calibration improves ordinal structure (d1--d3 BalAcc 40.4\\% vs.\\ 33.3\\% random) without closing the gap to RF (71.6\\%), confirming that the bottleneck is the input signal rather than the calibration strategy.\nPerturbation-based and membership-inference methods (28--33\\%) confirm that token-level detection scores lack ordinal depth resolution.\nCross-perplexity (Binoculars) is a notable exception: when evaluated across all 9 atlas cells with threshold calibration, Binoculars achieves 69.8\\%; with multi-feature RF calibration (11 cross-perplexity statistics), it reaches 78.1\\%, matching our 19-feature pipeline within 0.3\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Augmented feature set.}\nTo test whether detection scores carry complementary ordinal signal, we add Min-K\\% probability ($K{=}20$) and DetectGPT perturbation discrepancy as additional RF features.\nThe 21-feature model (19 + Min-K\\% + DetectGPT) achieves 78.90\\% mean 4-class accuracy (+0.48\\,pp) and 72.20\\% d1--d3 balanced accuracy (+0.60\\,pp).\nMin-K\\% ranks \\#3 by feature importance (0.095), behind \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (0.161) and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (0.126); DetectGPT ranks \\#17 (0.026).\nThe marginal improvement is consistent across 8 of 9 atlas cells, indicating that Min-K\\%'s token-level log-probability signal provides weak but real complementary information to the passage-level distributional features.\nThe top-2 features (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} + \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) retain combined importance of 28.7\\%, confirming that perplexity tail statistics remain the dominant depth markers even after augmentation.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise AUC and ordinal collapse.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:detector_pairwise_auc} reports pairwise binary AUC for three nucleus cells, contrasting our 19-feature RF with LR-calibrated detector scores and their combination.\nDetectors achieve reasonable d0-vs-d1 separation (Combined AUC\\,$\\geq$\\,0.949), but d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 AUC collapse toward chance (0.50--0.77), far below the RF pipeline (0.930--0.958 and 0.813--0.892 respectively).\nThis confirms that binary detection scores capture the real-vs-synthetic boundary but lack the ordinal gradient needed to distinguish recursive depths.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise binary AUC for detector baselines vs.\\ RF-19feat on three nucleus cells. RF uses 500-tree binary classifiers; detector methods use LR on continuous scores (Bino/FDGPT) or their combination. d1v2 and d2v3 AUC near 0.50 = ordinal collapse.}\n\\label{tab:detector_pairwise_auc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Method & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & RF-19feat & \\textbf{0.999} & \\textbf{0.930} & \\textbf{0.813} \\\\\n & Binoculars & 0.738 & 0.529 & 0.515 \\\\\n & Fast-DetectGPT & 0.797 & 0.596 & 0.530 \\\\\n & Combined & 0.990 & 0.767 & 0.626 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-1B & RF-19feat & \\textbf{0.997} & \\textbf{0.934} & \\textbf{0.819} \\\\\n & Binoculars & 0.744 & 0.509 & 0.522 \\\\\n & Fast-DetectGPT & 0.917 & 0.668 & 0.520 \\\\\n & Combined & 0.949 & 0.742 & 0.550 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL & RF-19feat & \\textbf{0.999} & \\textbf{0.958} & \\textbf{0.892} \\\\\n & Binoculars & 0.942 & 0.502 & 0.581 \\\\\n & Fast-DetectGPT & 0.955 & 0.644 & 0.655 \\\\\n & Combined & 0.969 & 0.724 & 0.703 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Binoculars Feature Ablation and Signal Decomposition}\n\\label{app:feature_ablation_bino}\n\nTo quantify the overlap and complementarity between our 19-feature pipeline and Binoculars' cross-perplexity features, we evaluate four feature configurations across all 9 atlas cells (Table~\\ref{tab:bino_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature ablation: overlap and merged configurations (9-cell mean, 5-fold CV, RF 200 trees). The 6 overlap features are the perplexity statistics shared between both pipelines (mean, variance, median, p10, p90, skewness).}\n\\label{tab:bino_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & \\#Feat. & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ vs.\\ 19-feat \\\\\n\\midrule\n6 overlap only & 6 & 75.9 & $-$2.5 \\\\\nOur 19 features & 19 & 78.4 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars 11 features & 11 & 78.1 & $-$0.3 \\\\\nMerged (19 + 11) & 30 & \\textbf{82.6} & +4.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 6 shared perplexity features alone achieve 75.9\\% (97\\% of the full pipeline's accuracy), confirming that mean-level perplexity distributional displacement is the core depth signal.\nOur 13 additional features (kurtosis, surprisal variance, lexical/n-gram statistics) contribute +2.5\\,pp of shape-level information; Binoculars' 5 unique features (observer-performer agreement score, cross-entropy statistics) contribute +4.2\\,pp of cross-model divergence when merged.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bino_ablation_percell} reports per-cell results for three configurations with per-cell breakdown (Bino-11 per-cell values are omitted because features were computed on the pooled 9-cell corpus).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-cell feature ablation (4-class accuracy \\%, 5-fold CV, RF 200 trees). $\\Delta$: merged minus 19-feature accuracy. Bino-11 per-cell values omitted because Binoculars features were computed on the pooled 9-cell corpus; the per-cell mean (78.1\\%) is reported in Table~\\ref{tab:bino_ablation}.}\n\\label{tab:bino_ablation_percell}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc c@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & 6-overlap & 19-feat & Bino-11 & Merged & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia nucleus & 76.7 & 78.7 & --- & 83.5 & +4.8 \\\\\nPythia temp09 & 73.9 & 76.8 & --- & 81.5 & +4.7 \\\\\nPythia top-$k$50 & 65.3 & 68.9 & --- & 71.2 & +2.3 \\\\\nOLMo nucleus & 76.4 & 78.9 & --- & 84.2 & +5.3 \\\\\nOLMo temp09 & 81.0 & 82.3 & --- & 88.3 & +6.0 \\\\\nOLMo top-$k$50 & 72.5 & 74.4 & --- & 78.4 & +4.0 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL nucleus & 81.7 & 84.3 & --- & 88.8 & +4.5 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL temp09 & 84.0 & 85.9 & --- & 88.5 & +2.6 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL top-$k$50 & 72.1 & 75.8 & --- & 79.1 & +3.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{9-cell mean} & \\textbf{75.9} & \\textbf{78.4} & \\textbf{78.1} & \\textbf{82.6} & \\textbf{+4.2} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-dependent complementarity.}\nThe merged improvement varies by decoding strategy: nucleus and temperature cells gain +4.7\\,pp on average, while top-k cells gain +3.2\\,pp.\nTop-k sampling truncates the token distribution to the $k$ highest-probability tokens, producing text with reduced distributional diversity; this limits the information available to cross-model divergence features, which rely on disagreement between observer and performer predictions.\nMerged improvement also varies by generator family: OLMo cells (observer-dissimilar) gain +5.1\\,pp on average, compared to +3.9\\,pp for Pythia (observer-similar) and +3.5\\,pp for GPT-2~XL, suggesting cross-model divergence features contribute more when observer and generator architectures differ.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scale Binoculars ablation (14B).}\nWe evaluate Binoculars cross-scale on two representative strategies (temperature, top-$k$); the nucleus cell was used for the main 14B pipeline and its GPU hours were allocated to generation rather than Binoculars evaluation.\nAt 14B, when only 1--1.5B reference models are available for cross-perplexity computation (Pythia-1.4B observer analyzing Qwen2.5-14B text), Binoculars features degrade substantially (Table~\\ref{tab:bino_14b_ablation}).\nSelf-reference statistical features (19-feat) outperform Binoculars (11-feat) by 8--11\\,pp, reversing the near-parity observed at 1--1.5B.\nMerging all 30 features yields a consistent +3.6--3.7\\,pp gain over the self-reference baseline across both tested strategies, confirming that cross-perplexity features retain complementary signal even under the 10$\\times$ scale gap, though the gain is attenuated relative to +4.2\\,pp at 1--1.5B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature ablation at 14B (Qwen2.5-14B, self-reference$^\\dagger$). Binoculars: observer=Pythia-1.4B, performer=Pythia-410M. $\\Delta$: merged minus main-pipeline stat-only accuracy (Method~A alignment). Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:bino_14b_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Bino-11 & Stat-19$^\\dagger$ & Merged-30 & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\ntemp09 & 42.6 & 50.5 & 54.2 & +3.7 \\\\\ntop-$k$50 & 37.7 & 47.9 & 51.5 & +3.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe Binoculars degradation reflects a practical deployment constraint: the 10$\\times$ scale gap between observer (1.4B) and generator (14B) causes cross-perplexity features to lose sensitivity, not a fundamental limitation of the cross-model divergence channel.\nSelf-reference features are more robust across scales because they avoid the observer--generator mismatch.\nThe accuracy difference between self-reference (50.8\\%) and Binoculars stat-only (40.1\\% mean) may therefore partially reflect observer capacity limitations rather than feature design differences.\n\n\\subsection{Prompted Generation Results}\n\\label{app:prompted_gen}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:prompted_gen} reports depth estimation performance under topic-seeded (prompted) generation using GPT-2~XL with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, depths 0--3).\nGeneration is conditioned on Wikipedia topic prompts rather than a bare BOS token, a more ecologically valid test of depth signal persistence.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Prompted generation (GPT-2~XL, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, topic-seeded). BOS baseline re-evaluated within the prompted-generation pipeline for matched comparison. 5-fold CV accuracy and pairwise AUC. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:prompted_gen}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMetric & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-class accuracy (\\%) & 69.52 $[68.9, 70.2]$ \\\\\nBOS-seeded baseline (\\%) & 83.3 \\\\\n$\\Delta$ (prompted $-$ BOS) & $-$13.78\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nAUC d0v1 & 0.916 \\\\\nAUC d1v2 & 0.782 \\\\\nAUC d2v3 & 0.744 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTop-1 feature & \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} \\\\\nTop-2 feature & \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} \\\\\nTop-3 feature & \\texttt{p75\\_ppl} \\\\\n\\bottomrule",

1102	
1103	The 13.8\,pp drop relative to BOS-seeded generation is expected: topic prompts constrain the output distribution, reducing the variance that distinguishes depths.
1104	Nevertheless, accuracy remains $2.8\times$ chance and pairwise AUC decays monotonically (0.916, 0.782, 0.744); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of unconstrained generation.
1105	Top features are identical to BOS-seeded (\texttt{p90\_ppl} first); the same perplexity tail erosion mechanism operates under prompted conditions.
1106	
1107	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
1108	\label{app:extended_discussion}
1109	
1110	\paragraph{Cross-strategy conditional decomposition.}
1111	The two-stage pipeline (strategy identification followed by depth classification) recovers 11.5\,pp over the na\"ive pooled classifier.
1112	Under correct strategy routing, four-class accuracy reaches 72.6\% vs.\ 68.0\% under misrouting ($N{=}103{,}498$ / $76{,}502$), a gap of 4.6\,pp; d0 accuracy remains near-ceiling (${\sim}99.6$\%) regardless of routing, confirming that the d0 signal is strategy-invariant.
1113	Even the worst routing yields a floor of 67.6\% ($2.7\times$ chance), and the 31.5\,pp d1--d3 gap between correct and wrong routing confirms feature-space misalignment rather than uniform noise.
1114	End-to-end PLMs lag RF by ${\sim}20$\,pp, suggesting that current pretrained representations do not capture the distributional tail statistics that drive depth discrimination.
1115	
1116	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation (inconclusive).}
1117	Initial curation experiments remain inconclusive: RF-vs-unfiltered is not significant ($p>0.4$; Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}).
1118	
1119	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
1120	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
1121	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
1122	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
1123	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
1124	A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
1125	
1126	\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}
1127	\label{app:compute}
1128	
1129	\paragraph{Hardware.}
1130	All experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\,GB) on cloud instances.
1131	Total computation is approximately 200 GPU$\cdot$hours, covering the $3 \times 3$ generation matrix (LoRA fine-tuning + sampling at each depth), feature extraction, depth extension to d5, 7--14B cross-scale experiments, and all ablations.
1132	
1133	\paragraph{Software.}
1134	Key library versions: PyTorch 2.10.0+cu128, Transformers 5.8.1, PEFT 0.7.1, scikit-learn 1.8.0.
1135	Additional dependencies: Accelerate 1.13.0, Datasets 4.8.4, XGBoost 3.2.0, NumPy 2.2.6 (Python 3.12.3, CUDA 12.8).
1136	
1137	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
1138	LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, lr $2{\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs) follow \citet{hu2022lora}.
1139	The Random Forest classifier uses 200 trees, Gini criterion, and no max depth, without hyperparameter search; all reported results use 5-fold stratified cross-validation.
1140	
1141	\paragraph{Inference cost.}
1142	Feature extraction requires a single forward pass of the reference model.
1143	On a single GPU this takes approximately 2 minutes for 5{,}000 passages; a CPU-only alternative requires approximately 50 minutes on server-grade hardware.
1144	RF classification adds negligible overhead ($<$1\,s).
1145	
1146	\section{Use of AI Assistants}
1147	\label{app:ai_assistants}
1148	
1149	The authors used AI-based writing assistants, including Claude, for limited support during manuscript preparation, including grammar polishing, wording suggestions, LaTeX editing, and code/debugging assistance. The tools were not used to generate the core research ideas, experimental design, methodological decisions, data analysis, or scientific conclusions. All AI-assisted text, code suggestions, and references were manually reviewed and edited by the authors.

      "content": "\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 13.8\\,pp drop relative to BOS-seeded generation is expected: topic prompts constrain the output distribution, reducing the variance that distinguishes depths.\nNevertheless, accuracy remains $2.8\\times$ chance and pairwise AUC decays monotonically (0.916, 0.782, 0.744); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of unconstrained generation.\nTop features are identical to BOS-seeded (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} first); the same perplexity tail erosion mechanism operates under prompted conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy conditional decomposition.}\nThe two-stage pipeline (strategy identification followed by depth classification) recovers 11.5\\,pp over the na\\\"ive pooled classifier.\nUnder correct strategy routing, four-class accuracy reaches 72.6\\% vs.\\ 68.0\\% under misrouting ($N{=}103{,}498$ / $76{,}502$), a gap of 4.6\\,pp; d0 accuracy remains near-ceiling (${\\sim}99.6$\\%) regardless of routing, confirming that the d0 signal is strategy-invariant.\nEven the worst routing yields a floor of 67.6\\% ($2.7\\times$ chance), and the 31.5\\,pp d1--d3 gap between correct and wrong routing confirms feature-space misalignment rather than uniform noise.\nEnd-to-end PLMs lag RF by ${\\sim}20$\\,pp, suggesting that current pretrained representations do not capture the distributional tail statistics that drive depth discrimination.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation (inconclusive).}\nInitial curation experiments remain inconclusive: RF-vs-unfiltered is not significant ($p>0.4$; Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}).\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n\n\\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}\n\\label{app:compute}\n\n\\paragraph{Hardware.}\nAll experiments were conducted on 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) on cloud instances.\nTotal computation is approximately 200 GPU$\\cdot$hours, covering the $3 \\times 3$ generation matrix (LoRA fine-tuning + sampling at each depth), feature extraction, depth extension to d5, 7--14B cross-scale experiments, and all ablations.\n\n\\paragraph{Software.}\nKey library versions: PyTorch 2.10.0+cu128, Transformers 5.8.1, PEFT 0.7.1, scikit-learn 1.8.0.\nAdditional dependencies: Accelerate 1.13.0, Datasets 4.8.4, XGBoost 3.2.0, NumPy 2.2.6 (Python 3.12.3, CUDA 12.8).\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\nLoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs) follow \\citet{hu2022lora}.\nThe Random Forest classifier uses 200 trees, Gini criterion, and no max depth, without hyperparameter search; all reported results use 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Inference cost.}\nFeature extraction requires a single forward pass of the reference model.\nOn a single GPU this takes approximately 2 minutes for 5{,}000 passages; a CPU-only alternative requires approximately 50 minutes on server-grade hardware.\nRF classification adds negligible overhead ($<$1\\,s).\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistants}\n\\label{app:ai_assistants}\n\nThe authors used AI-based writing assistants, including Claude, for limited support during manuscript preparation, including grammar polishing, wording suggestions, LaTeX editing, and code/debugging assistance. The tools were not used to generate the core research ideas, experimental design, methodological decisions, data analysis, or scientific conclusions. All AI-assisted text, code suggestions, and references were manually reviewed and edited by the authors.\n",

    }
